Trump’s Dream World in Dallas
2026-09-10

Trump’s Dream World in Dallas

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
President Trump began his midterm-convention address last night by casting his spell on the crowd in Dallas. “This place is packed,” he announced from the stage, pointing to the back of the American Airlines Center, where everyone could see hundreds of empty seats. Those who looked up saw thousands more, whole sections without any people at all. But the crowd cheered anyway. A deal had been struck. Reality would be ignored. After more than a decade in politics, Trump is facing his last great electoral test—his popularity is waning, his party is down in the polls, Republicans could lose control in the U.S. House and maybe the Senate. He demanded greater complicity minutes later, seeming to invent a person in the back of the room. “He’s leaving for the bathroom. He’ll be back in two minutes,” he said, pointing. “They’ll take a picture of his seat while he’s gone. And they’ll say Trump did not fill up the arena.” Laughter joined with the cheers for their political wizard. It was working. Trump could say that he had passed “the largest tax cut in the history of our country,” even if that was not the case. He could claim to have won the presidency three times, when he’s actually done so twice. He could describe the recent past as “the most successful two years financially and every other way in the history of the presidency,” despite six in 10 voters saying he had made the economy worse. He could promise to pay every adult U.S. citizen $5,000 if Republicans held both legislative houses, as if that sum did not amount to about 80 percent of the federal discretionary budget. He might even be able to convince the country to embrace the big fantasy, a chance to defy history and seal victory for Republicans in November. “I’m asking you to pretend that I’m on the ballot,” he said. “I’m on the ballot. I’m on the ballot. Just one more time.” The night was all about Trump. He was the alpha and omega, the focus and the solution. Attendees wore pictures of the president on lanyards around their necks, the ticket for entry to the arena. VIP boxes were stocked with Trump was right about everything hats. Oversize photos of the president lined the hallways. Five hours before he arrived, he received his first standing ovation. A video montage played on the big screen, images of the president narrated by a reading of Rudyard Kipling’s “If”—“If you can talk with crowds and keep your virtue.” When each speaker dropped Trump’s name, it was cause for riotous celebration. Until now, national political conventions have been held only during presidential-election years, when delegates from each state gather to select their nominee. In the 1970s, the gatherings shifted from smoke-filled rooms for party business to televised infomercials for the candidates. Trump this year created a simulation of a simulation. The balloon nets had no nominee to drop for. The state delegations, sitting under signs on the floor, had no procedural purpose. (The smart party leaders, one strategist told us, were selling their “delegate” tickets to donors.) The convention-in-name-only was for, by, and about one man—who, importantly, cannot be a candidate for president again, according to the U.S. Constitution. Read: Trump plans to buy midterm ads about his own record “The only way to keep dangerous, radical, weird out of the Senate is a united Republican Party standing with our president, Donald J. Trump,” Senator Tim Scott of South Carolina, the chair of the National Republican Senatorial Committee, bellowed from the stage. “Stand for the president, Donald J. Trump. This is a battle for the heart of our nation. I said the heart of our nation.” No president has tried something like this before. None has had so much money to spend on themselves at this point in a presidency. None has chosen to make the midterms a referendum on themselves while polling so badly. But this is Trump, and Trump has come back from worse. For a decade, the GOP’s electoral success has risen and fallen on his energies. The strategist in chief has bet that turning out Trump voters, the ones who typically stay home when he is not on the ballot, is the only path forward. This is why the first ads he bought for the cycle were not about any candidates who are running this year, but about himself. This was why his latest show was a celebration of the unlimited possibility of his incantations. “Vote Republican, and we will leave the world in our dust for generations to come,” he told the crowd. “There’ll be nothing like it.” Outside Trump’s curated fantasy world, not everything has gone according to plan. Trump’s political strategist, James Blair, announced after the off-year elections in 2025 that the president would be “very, very focused on prices and cost of living.” White House aides told us in the new year that the pivot would come after Trump scratched his itch for another confrontation with Iran. By May, as that conflict dragged on, Trump advisers told us quietly that if it had not receded, along with gas prices, by Labor Day, there would be trouble. Two weeks ago, multiple Trump advisers told us they expected the kinetic part of the Iran war to mostly cease in September, clearing the way for lower gas prices. None of that has happened. Missiles are still flying in the Strait of Hormuz. Since the 2025 elections, gas prices have risen 39 percent, from $3.08 to $4.28; 30-year mortgage rates have increased 8 percent; electricity prices have gone up more than 4 percent. Charlie Cook, a political analyst and the dean emeritus of legislative-election handicapping, says that Republicans should brace themselves for a tough result. “The worst-case scenario for the GOP and best for Democrats today would have sounded ludicrous eight months ago,” he wrote recently. “The most plausible today resembles Republicans’ nightmare scenario at the beginning of the year.” Those Republican nightmares, both new and old, are unlikely to be worse than the 40-seat gain made by Democrats in the House of Representatives during Trump’s first term, in 2018, thanks to increased polarization and mid-decennial redistricting. But few Republicans still pretend off-camera that they will be able to maintain control of the House. Narrow Democratic control of the Senate, once outlandish, could be within reach. On the sidelines of the convention in Dallas, strategists muttered about recent polling in House races that shows jump balls where Trump won by double digits in 2024. The outstanding question is whether Democratic candidates can emerge from the dim shadow of President Biden. “While there is a lot of focus on the Republicans’ problems, Democrats have a real problem—they have a real party-image problem,” the Trump pollster Tony Fabrizio told us. Jesse Ferguson, a Democratic strategist, depicts his party in PowerPoint presentations as a backpacker hiking up a steep mountain with gusts of wind at his back. The incline represents Trump’s financial advantages, and the challenge of winning seats in gerrymandered districts and Republican states such as Ohio, Iowa, Texas, and Alaska. The wind is voter sentiment and the enthusiasm advantage inside the Democratic Party.  “Democrats have won the message fight. Republicans have lost the governance fight,” he told us. “The question is whether they can use structural advantages to overcome it.” On the whole, Democrats are delighted by Trump’s strategy—to put front and center a man whose unpopularity fires up their voters and further alienates the newly skeptical. “If the Democrats have a great year, it will be because they got their base out to vote in large numbers,” Rich Thau, the moderator of the Swing Voter Project, told us. “It won’t be because they magically converted these swing voters into Democratic senatorial and gubernatorial voters.” But they may not be accounting for Trump’s magic. Two years ago, he bounced back from two impeachments and an attack by his supporters on the U.S. Capitol to win the presidency. Now the odds are against him again. By multiple measures, the president who appeared on the Dallas stage last night had lost a step. When he first won the White House, in 2016, he denounced the folly of distant wars of choice, promised to remedy the reigning economic turmoil of globalization, and boasted of his independence as someone who eschewed campaign donations. A decade later, he is managing a reescalating war in Iran that he started. He has positioned himself as a champion of data centers, the latest lightning rod for populist resentment. And he controls a massive $850 million slush fund he acquired in office through seemingly transactional demands of companies, lobbyists, and wealthy individuals seeking government favor. His economic approval rating this summer, according to Quinnipiac University, stood at 34 percent, down from 53 percent on the eve of the last midterm elections on his watch, in 2018. The 2024 presidential election hinged on the rising cost of living, and after Republicans won the trifecta—the White House and both chambers of Congress—the expectation was that Trump and his party would fix what many still view as Biden’s economy. “I want to emphasize that this wasn’t to just slightly improve things,” David Winston, a Republican strategist who works with congressional leadership, told us. “They viewed a substantive outcome as what they were looking for.” It has not come to pass. Trump has not lost his talent for showmanship. No longer tied to a single hit program in a single slot on NBC, he has spent much of the year creating television spectacles from the Oval Office—a Formula One race, a UFC fight, a Fourth of July party, a World Cup championship celebration where he lingered in the frame. This string of network debuts gave him the confidence to schedule his convention against the first two games of the NFL season. Only Trump would choose to go up against the Seattle Seahawks and New England Patriots. For those who watched, the first night in Dallas had production quality that has escaped many recent Republican conventions. All the tropes and tricks were hit—the testimonials of a Pennsylvania steelworker and a Florida grandma about policy wins, the glitter reels of alleged Democratic bad behavior, the plea for campaign donations for Ken Paxton, the Republican Senate nominee from Texas. Multiple speakers gave mock “land acknowledgements” praising the national heritage of European colonizers. The comedian Adam Carolla did a bit about Trump’s hair: “You ever see underwater footage of a kelp bed?” There were “everyday Americans,” “American workers,” and Border Patrol agents who gathered onstage, many in work gear. “President Trump refuses to surrender,” Senator Dave McCormick of Pennsylvania said, after a friendly video introduction from his Democratic colleague John Fetterman. “This November there can be no surrender.” There was even a moment to lay out the core contrast that Trump’s advisers hope will frame the election, the sort of message that any other Republican president in his position would be entirely focused on delivering at this point. In a short video, his team spliced together clips from fringe voices on the left—the “woke,” the America-bashing, the revolutionary—before defining the decision on the ballot. “This is no longer simply Republican versus Democrat, or conservative versus liberal,” said the voice-over. “Today the choice is unmistakable: extreme versus mainstream.” But the centerpiece remained Trump. Speaker after speaker described him in near-messianic terms. “As it turns out, Washington wasn’t incapable of working for the American people; all we needed was a new president,” Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said. T. W. Shannon, a candidate for lieutenant governor in Oklahoma, placed the entire GOP in the president’s shadow. “We’re also the party of the greatest president in American history, President Donald J. Trump,” he declared. But no comments mattered more than Trump’s. He talked for nearly two hours, a rolicking, weaving address that only occasionally stuck to the teleprompter. It ended where he began. “Please pretend that I’m running, and get out and vote on November 3 or sooner,” he said. “Because, in actuality, I really am running.” In actuality, he is not. Or maybe he is, in a way. His crowd remained in their seats to the end. The room, at times, felt packed to the rafters. Additional reporting by Jack Rodriquez-Vars
The Atlantic
What Russia Is Really Up To in Europe
2026-09-10

What Russia Is Really Up To in Europe

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
On Russian state television, the Kremlin’s propagandists have for years threatened to bomb European capitals, even while the country’s diplomats insist that no such war is imminent. Lately, the denials have gotten hard to sustain, as European intelligence agencies have ascribed hundreds of clandestine sabotage operations—cyberattacks in Italy, arson in the United Kingdom, undersea-cable ruptures in the Baltic, severed pig heads left outside French mosques—to Russia. U.S. intelligence assessments conclude that a conventional Russian attack on a NATO country is likely to happen sometime from this fall to 2029. But another possibility is that Moscow will remain content with sabotage and intimidation. This hybrid campaign is all the war Russia wants or needs—and stoking fear that Moscow just might directly strike Ukraine’s European allies is part of it. If this is what Russian President Vladimir Putin is really up to in Europe, then he was telling the truth when he assured Trump this week that Russia has “no aggressive intentions against European statehood.” Moscow has not massed troops on any NATO member’s border. Rather, it has been sending a carefully calibrated message meant to persuade Europe to stop supporting Ukraine. For Moscow, this has been a summer of setbacks in Ukraine. Russia stalled on the battlefield and sustained damage from thousands of Ukrainian long-range drones that plunged the country into fuel shortages. And so it escalated elsewhere in Europe: Fires broke out at ammunition plants in Italy, Bulgaria, and Estonia last month, and explosive-laden drones were discovered near a German power plant and at an airport in Leipzig, apparently intended to destroy Ukrainian cargo planes. From the Kremlin’s point of view, Europe has been deepening its involvement in Ukraine. At the beginning of the full-scale war, Europe donated weapons to Ukraine from its own stocks. Within two years, European governments were paying Ukrainian factories to manufacture arms. Last year, Fire Point, a Ukrainian missile-and-drone manufacturer, announced that it would establish a solid-fuel production plant in Denmark, making it the first such arms company to expand production abroad. Today, Europe provides the bulk of financial assistance to Kyiv. It has also become Ukraine’s secure production base—Ukrainian weapons are manufactured in Germany, Britain, the Netherlands, Norway, Sweden, France, Romania, and Spain. Because Kyiv is attacking Russia’s rear, it’s logical to worry that Moscow will attack Ukraine’s. Russian security services are almost certainly capable of carrying out a lethal attack on European soil. And Western war games have entertained many possibilities along those lines. Among them are a limited Russian incursion into a small Baltic town that has a Russian-speaking population; a full-scale invasion of the Baltic states, launched simultaneously from Belarus and Kaliningrad, Russia’s European exclave, to cut them off from the rest of Europe; the rapid creation of an impenetrable drone “kill zone” on European soil; and a strategic blockade of the Baltic Sea. Some of these scenarios are plausible; others would require hundreds of thousands of Russian troops to crisscross Europe. Russia has not visibly prepared for any such scenario, however. Instead, it continues to wage its hybrid war—which European security services continue to thwart. Such are the headlines: Drones carrying explosives are discovered in Leipzig, improvised explosive devices are found near a German power plant, and flying objects are spotted near airports. Moscow may not much care that these operations get foiled. In a way, the effect is similar to that of Russia’s harassment of journalists and embassy workers stationed in Moscow: Things in their apartment get rearranged, signs of eavesdropping are deliberately made obvious, or a window is left open in the middle of winter. Such interference conveys a sinister message: We’re watching you. We have access to you. You are not in control. But it’s still different from blowing up the apartment. Putin has generally chosen to fully engage the Russian military only in conflicts that he feels (rightly or, in the case of Ukraine, wrongly) assured of winning. This does not describe any hypothetical attack on the Baltic states, which have heavily fortified borders, conduct regular military exercises, and spend 5 percent of their GDP on defense. That’s not to mention the thousands of British, German, and Canadian troops stationed near the Baltics as part of NATO’s Enhanced Forward Presence. Moscow may be willing to wager that the United States won’t intervene should Russia attack Estonia. But Russia would then find itself at war with numerous European states armed with submarines, aircraft carriers, fifth-generation fighter jets, long-range cruise missiles, and even nuclear weapons—all while it was still fighting in Ukraine. Russia’s hybrid war has not succeeded in its likely purpose: stopping weapons, money, and intelligence from flowing into Ukraine from Europe. Kyiv has more arms, funds, and—judging by its strikes on Russian soil—intelligence than ever before. Neither has the Russian campaign taken out a European satellite that supplies Ukraine with intelligence, say, or destroyed a shipment of Patriot missiles. But Putin persists in this campaign for a reason. Through inexpensive means, expendable operatives, and a steady stream of chilling leaks, the Kremlin has millions of Westerners braced for it to wage a full-scale war against Europe. Regardless of whether that war is likely, Putin will be demanding concessions in exchange for bringing it to an end.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine
This Trump Speech Sent a Dangerous Message to Iran—and the Rest of the World
2026-09-10

This Trump Speech Sent a Dangerous Message to Iran—and the Rest of the World

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Sometimes I wonder whether President Trump simply doesn’t think before he speaks or whether he has a weird compulsion to blow up the world. It happened again Wednesday during his harangue at the Republican Midterm Convention, which was really a fancy name for a Trump campaign rally. Amid the usual rants of self-puffery and other fictions, he said this: If [Iran] had a nuclear weapon, I’d be calling the Supreme Leader and I’d be saying, ‘Mr. Supreme Leader, how are you, sir? Is there anything we can do for you?’—as opposed to bombing the crap out of him. Many in the audience laughed and applauded, when they should have gasped or moaned. Trump added, “We cannot let them have a nuclear weapon, it’s very simple”—as if that were the lesson of his little tale. But the lesson that any world leader—friend, foe, or in between—would draw from this speech is simpler still: I need to get some nuclear weapons as quickly as possible. Trump is telling the world’s leaders that he might bomb the crap out of them at any moment, or treat them like crap as a whim—but he would kiss the hems of their garments, bestow them with whatever favors they request, if they got themselves some nukes. Maybe this is why he treats Russia’s Vladimir Putin, China’s Xi Jinping, and North Korea’s Kim Jong-un with such respect. Maybe it’s not just that he envies their absolute power on the home front or their unabashedly lavish palaces. Maybe it’s also because, like him, they have enough nuclear bombs to destroy much of life on earth—and he fears they might do so unless he showers them with praise and honor. If this is part of his reasoning, he’s making two mistakes. First, he assumes that they’re as gullible as he is—that, since he believes the compliments they shovel his way, they’ll believe his shovelings too. This almost certainly is not the case; they are much more hardheaded and cynical in their views of the world and their interests. Second, he apparently doesn’t consider what anyone outside the realm of his political base might think. He probably would treat Iran’s leaders differently—certainly he wouldn’t bombard their country with no good reason—if they had a pocketful of nukes to fire back at him. That’s what “nuclear deterrence” is all about; the possession of nuclear weapons deters others from behaving aggressively. But a leader—especially the president of the United States—should know better than to say such things out loud. Doing so flashes a message in bright neon: You want to block me from bombing your country? You want me to be nice? Then go get yourself some nuclear weapons. To the extent Iran still has the technology and the chops to build some A-bombs, you can bet that its scientists are revving up the centrifuges to further enrich their remaining stocks of uranium—while they still can. Kim Jong-un long ago passed this hurdle—which, many have already concluded, is why Iran gets bombed while North Korea’s far more monstrous regime is tolerated, even spoken of respectfully. But to the extent Kim responds to other leaders’ signals, Trump’s comment may move him to accelerate the expansion of Pyongyang’s arsenal. Meanwhile, many of our allies—notably South Korea, Japan, and a few countries in Europe—will no doubt absorb the same message. They have already been thinking about abandoning their restraint when it comes to going nuclear. They have the technical capacity to make the leap, they live in an increasingly dangerous world, they no longer trust the United States to come to their aid in a crisis. And now Trump is loudly confirming the correlation they’ve considered but would rather not think too hard about, until now—that a nuclear arsenal can win them safety and honor from even the most disreputable foes. And when one country goes nuclear, the dominoes start to fall. If Iran builds nukes, Saudi Arabia would certainly follow suit. The royal family has said as much. If South Korea or Japan develops their own deterrent, China will accelerate their own deployments in order to overwhelm their deterrent. Regional arms races worldwide will ensue. There is a school of thought—an extreme branch of “International Realism”—that views nuclear proliferation as a peace-building process: the more nations that have nuclear bombs, the less likely their adversaries will start war. Maybe. But maybe not, even stipulating their premise that all leaders (including savage and adventurous ones) act rationally. New and overly eager nuclear powers might not take the time and care to erect the guardrails that the larger powers gradually installed over the decades—the spy satellites that gauge the size of an opponent’s arsenal, the early-warning sensors that detect an incoming attack, the command-control machinery that ensures only those authorized to push the button have the ability to do so. Even the superpowers’ guardrails—built and maintained at great expense during the Cold War—have failed at times, the radar mistaking flocks of geese for an ICBM attack or a computer mistaking a prerehearsed simulation for the real thing. At least the U.S. and Russia, oceans apart from each other, had half an hour to sort out the confusion before the missiles would land if they had been real. Countries much closer to each other might not have the time. In the real world, as opposed to in academic theory, a world bubbling over with nuclear weapons from many stovetops is more dangerous—not less. Meanwhile, almost every time Trump talks, he sets off another problem, at times to the point of lighting a book of matches or pouring gasoline on a looming fire, often without seeing what he’s doing, without considering that words have consequences. We are learning, more and more, that this is what happens when someone of Trump’s character becomes president. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Can Trump Buy Back Americans’ Love at $5,000 a Piece? Guess We’ll Find Out.
2026-09-10

Can Trump Buy Back Americans’ Love at $5,000 a Piece? Guess We’ll Find Out.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The first Republican midterm convention had all the simulacra of a real political convention. There were seats assigned by state and indicated by tall signs on poles, there was a host of precisely timed and deeply forgettable speeches by elected officials, and, of course, there was a keynote speaker. But those were all window dressing to hide what it really was: just another Trump rally. There was news—or at least an eye-popping statement designed to make headlines: Donald Trump promised that, so long as Republicans retained control of both the House and Senate, every American would receive a $5,000 check from the U.S. government. It was a statement perfectly calibrated the next day in newspapers and local news broadcasts. That does not, however, mean it’s likely to happen. Republicans are a long shot to keep the House and far from a lock to hold on to the Senate. There are obvious policy issues with this, as it would cost over a trillion dollars. There are obvious political issues with it: Trump has had issues trying to rally fiscally conservative Republicans behind far less ambitious agenda items. It also was perfectly hedged as a proposal, conditioned only on the scenario that the GOP manages to defy political gravity in November. Still, it’s something new, at a time when Trump’s “Lake America”–style gimmicks have gone stale—a replacement to standard promises from Trump’s first term about building a wall on the U.S.–Mexico border, ending foreign wars, and having so much winning that “your head will spin.” For those more likely to hear about politics from eight-second TikTok clips than 800-word news articles, Trump made masturbation jokes. The speakers preceding him were disjointed and all making their own pitches for why Democrats were bad and Republicans were good. Some touted the merits of last year’s Republican spending bill, now on its third or fourth name ahead of the midterms. Others focused on the evils of Democrats with the remarks of far-left streamer Hasan Piker, who has become a surrogate for a number of Democratic candidates, receiving particular attention. Some were candidates themselves, delivering versions of their own stump speech, just like the array of speakers who proceed Trump at every rally as a way to tide over the restless crowd before the main event. (The most noteworthy speaker before Trump was a recorded video from John Fetterman, who introduced his Republican colleague Dave McCormick. The iconoclastic Pennsylvanian did not back Trump in remarks that seemed designed more to influence Sunday-show bookers than Keystone State swing voters.) And just as there are occasional celebrity cameos at a Trump rally, there was one at the midterm convention, where early-aughts comic Adam Carolla did a routine. When Trump finally took the stage, he spoke for nearly two hours, just like a rally. In the front of the crowd were many of the usual Trump superfans who flock to every rally in the same way that fans of the Grateful Dead used to follow the rock group across the country. The president even shouted them out as he spoke—“the Front-Row Joes” and, of course, “Brick Suit,” a MAGA devotee who wears a suit with a brick pattern on it to show his ardent support for building a border wall with Mexico. The speech itself often felt like a rally speech. It was filled with digressions (Trump took shots at targets ranging from a New York Times writer to Michael Dukakis), and often the president spent an extended stretch as a fainthearted surrogate, urging voters to support Texas Senate nominee Ken Paxton, despite the fact that Paxton dresses badly. But, of course, a mega Trump rally is what the GOP needs right now. Part of the modern role of political conventions is to fix the fractures that emerge within a political party during a presidential primary and signify that everyone is united in a common task. In Trump’s GOP, that’s not an issue. While a handful of moderate dissidents are tacitly tolerated as a matter of expedience, most other heretics have been driven from the tent. The party is united around Trump; the challenge is whether a significant portion of Republicans will vote for anyone besides Trump. The traditional utility of Trump rallies in competitive elections is to energize the MAGA base. At a time when Republican enthusiasm is lagging, that’s what this was about. As Trump told viewers that his wish was for voters to pretend that his name was on the ballot, this was about trying to energize his diehard loyalists—the people who were ready to storm the Capitol on Jan. 6 but didn’t bother to show up in the voting booth in 2022—to turn out in November. Trump rallies have long been effective at this in specific races—Trump’s recent appearance in Myrtle Beach before a primary election in South Carolina may have been enough to steer that race to his preferred candidate—but this was just one big nationalized Trump rally. It might help some, but for anyone hoping it’ll swing the midterms, it probably wouldn’t be prudent to bet an entire $5,000 check. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The IRS Gave ICE Some Information It Really Shouldn’t Have. There Will Be Consequences for All of Us.
2026-09-10

The IRS Gave ICE Some Information It Really Shouldn’t Have. There Will Be Consequences for All of Us.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
This is Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Click here to receive it in your inbox each week. The IRS arguably holds Americans’ most sensitive information, more so than any other federal agency, and for the better part of 2025, it quietly handed over that information, to the tune of about 47,000 records, to the Department of Homeland Security. This was done to aid in President Donald Trump’s deportation agenda, but that doesn’t mean it was legal: An appeals court this week found that the IRS violated federal law when it turned over the addresses of folks it deemed to lack permanent legal status. Now the agency and the employees who participated in the illicit information sharing could face civil and criminal consequences, including lawsuits from people who were deported after the IRS delivered their information to DHS. The IRS is just one agency that got roped into cooperating with the Trump administration amid its immigration crackdown last year. Under normal circumstances, the IRS is forbidden from disclosing Americans’ tax information to anyone, including to other federal agencies and even within the executive branch, something Congress codified into federal law in the aftermath of Watergate. However, there are a few narrow exceptions, including active non-tax-related criminal investigations. This is where Trump’s DHS began scheming, crafting a memorandum of understanding that took advantage of a loophole in a federal statute. The memorandum stated that the IRS could provide undocumented taxpayers’ addresses because a person is technically subject to criminal investigation if they remain in the U.S. beyond 90 days after receiving a final order of removal. The MOU was so controversial it drove former Acting IRS Commissioner Melanie Krause, whom Trump appointed in early 2025, to resign in protest last April, alongside other top IRS leadership. Nevertheless, (now) former Immigration and Customs Enforcement Director Todd Lyons wasted no time after the IRS deal was struck, requesting the last known addresses of 1.28 million undocumented individuals late that June. By July 1, the IRS was giving its employees the green light to process Lyons’ requests. However, according to the U.S. Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit, per federal law, that blanket request shouldn’t have been honored. Instead, ICE should have provided the IRS with specific information about its criminal investigations before the IRS shared any of its records, including the addresses DHS had on file for the specified individuals; however, that did not happen for more than 90 percent of DHS’s records requests. As the appeals court concluded, the IRS “never confirmed whether ICE request contained information plausibly reflecting ‘the name and address of the taxpayer.’ ” In fact, the judges suggested, ICE could have submitted requests with addresses like “ ‘Don’t Care 12345,’ or ‘00000,’ and still received a taxpayer’s address.” That’s not all. If it had followed the law, ICE would have identified a point of contact at the agency for each taxpayer’s criminal investigation. Instead, the court found, Lyons was listed for all 1.28 million records requests—which is “facially impossible,” the judges wrote. The agency also failed to provide a reason why a taxpayer’s address would be relevant to its investigation. The bottom line is that the IRS dramatically moved away from its long-standing policy of closely guarding taxpayers’ sensitive information and reviewing records requests on a case-by-case basis to “a mass, automated review of millions of records at the press of a button.” What happens now? For its actions, the government could “face steep civil and criminal consequences for willful disclosure of information,” in violation of federal tax law—but it’s largely up to Congress to decide if and how to exact those consequences. The House Ways and Means Committee or the Senate Finance Committee could hold oversight hearings to dig into what occurred here and chose to penalize the IRS by pulling its funding or imposing guardrails on its work. The appetite to do so likely depends largely on whether Democrats retake the House in the upcoming midterms. Even more significantly, everyone who was swept up in DHS’s probe of addresses and was deported or subjected to ICE because the IRS handed over their tax records now has standing to sue. It’s not clear how many individuals were deported directly from the two agencies’ MOU, but we do know that arrests and deportations across the country have skyrocketed. However, those who were directly affected and are no longer on U.S. soil likely face an uphill battle to pursue legal action against the Trump administration. There’s another way this could all come back to bite the IRS. The agency has for decades carefully built up trust within America’s immigrant population by pushing them to file their taxes, no matter their status, and promising that this will not trigger deportation. With a new tax season beginning on Jan. 1, “some people will be wary of filing their tax returns,” Martin Sheil, a former IRS investigator and supervisory special agent for the agency’s Criminal Investigation Division, told me. “From an IRS perspective, it is their mission to ensure and promote voluntary compliance with the Internal Revenue Code.” The whole situation underscores the IRS’s politically fraught mission, which for decades, Sheil said, heavily discouraged its employees from making any illegal disclosures—even inadvertent ones—because people need to feel confident that their tax records are being protected. “Then you get into a wrestling match between agencies and mission priorities,” Sheil said. “ICE feels like their No. 1 one priority, laid out by the president, is to scoop up illegal immigrants, and they want to use any means necessary to assist them in meeting their mission priority.” But all federal agencies are at risk if Americans suddenly stop complying with the IRS—a trend that should majorly worry Congress. Maintaining the confidence of taxpayers is integral to keeping the federal government’s lights on, particularly when you consider that undocumented workers pay an estimated $90 billion in federal, state, and local taxes, despite being ineligible for most social programs, including Social Security and Medicare. Even a 1-percentage-point loss among the overall U.S. population would slash federal revenue by $40 billion. Over the past year, everyone in the U.S., regardless of legal status, has personally witnessed or read about ICE’s heavy-handed tactics across the country and is now learning about the unlawful relationship the IRS struck up with DHS. That could cumulatively inhibit people from complying with tax law, and this ought to alarm Congress, the body that has not just the authority to step in and investigate how the IRS and DHS have been working together, but an obligation to do so. We hope you learned a thing or two from this edition of Executive Dysfunction. If you enjoyed reading it, please consider supporting our legal journalism by becoming a Slate Plus member! The episode also delves into how the court’s conservative majority uses doctrinal head-fakes to ensure that the president always wins in court. In the Amicus bonus episode, Madiba K. Dennie, of Balls and Strikes, joins Dahlia to unpack the justices’ attempts to prevent their papers from going public and analyzes the broader message that sends about SCOTUS. House GOP Leader Mike Johnson failed to pass an amendment that would cap the number of Supreme Court justices at nine. Friend of Slate Alexis Romero, an economic and environmental justice attorney, argues that this cynical move demonstrates that “Republicans are so scared of the electorate voting for judicial reform” that they are willing to try to sign away their own authority on the matter. Trump’s executive order commanding the U.S. Postal Service to suppress mail voting is not actually the first time the federal government has attempted to control what gets mailed to Americans. Friend of Slate Steven Lubet, the Williams Memorial Professor Emeritus at Northwestern University Pritzker School of Law, explains how USPS was similarly weaponized after a postmaster refused to mail pamphlets highlighting the human-rights abuses perpetuated by slavery. The trial of Lindsay Clancy, the high-profile murder case that consumed the nation for weeks, ended in a mistrial last Friday. The jury was held up by a single juror, who refused to join his 11 fellow jurors in finding Clancy not guilty. I spoke with a trial attorney to understand how common a hung jury really is, why the judge in this case decided not to throw out the one unruly juror, and what the prosecution should do next. Trump has continuously violated the rights and interests of individual states for his personal and partisan political interests, an impeachable offense, argues friend of Slate Frank Bowman, a law professor and a former federal and state prosecutor. The president has specifically gone after blue states, following an unprecedented pattern that amounts to “an abuse of presidential power sufficiently egregious and disruptive of constitutional order to merit impeachment,” Bowman writes. The Surprising Moment MAGA’s Hold on America Broke
2026-09-10

The Surprising Moment MAGA’s Hold on America Broke

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Exactly one year ago, on a warm afternoon in north-central Utah, conservative activist Charlie Kirk was shot and killed, allegedly by a 22-year-old assassin who had set up a makeshift sniper’s nest on a nearby rooftop. Kirk was in the middle of one of the campus debate shows that had become his calling card during his decade-long career as a MAGA agitator, and as such, disturbing videos of the killing quickly spread across social media. If you’ve never seen the footage, I don’t recommend seeking it out. Kirk lolls his head back in a sickeningly unnatural motion; blood gushes from his neck; the crowd screams and scatters. It’s an astonishing act of violence, and Kirk was officially pronounced dead at the hospital two hours later. In the aftermath of the assassination, the MAGA commentariat instinctively sainted the newly martyred Kirk. There was good reason for this: Kirk was a victim of political violence, and victims of political violence are generally reified in the public record. At long last, Donald Trump had a bloody shirt to wave about. But when I survey the legacy of that fateful day in Utah from the vantage point of 2026, something else becomes clear. What conservatives thought they might harvest from the death of Kirk—a soft-power cultural mandate, broad bipartisan empathy, perhaps an assertion that it was liberals who were the real psychopaths in the room—never came to pass. Instead, ironically, I’ve concluded that Kirk’s killing represented the tipping point of Peak MAGAdom, a breaking of a certain fever. It had been less than a year since Trump had retaken the presidency—and unlike in 2017, in 2025 the masses were grappling with whether Trump’s movement was culturally ascendent. Kirk’s murder proved that it was not. How am I so sure about this? Well, for one thing, on the first anniversary of Charlie Kirk’s death, he has become, by far, the biggest meme on the internet. Where do we even start? How about the most offensive thing I’ve ever seen—we can work backward from there. Here is an A.I.-hewn video of Charlie Kirk at that pivotal Utah debate show. Portentous clouds gather overhead. The bullet is fired, and, miraculously, it is caught by someone inches away from its target. The camera pulls back to reveal who Kirk’s savior is. It’s Jeffrey Epstein. He and Kirk form a conga line in celebration. (This rendering was posted by X user @GlobalNewsMontr. As of this writing, it has been viewed more than 1.3 million times.) Zooming out further, here is a subreddit—called r/kirkified—that was created on Nov. 2, 2025, less than two months after the assassination. Inside, there are hundreds—thousands?—of images edited to paste Kirk’s distinctive gestalt into otherwise unrelated scenes. Here is Kirk as Napoleon Bonaparte. Here is Kirk as Gordon Freeman from the video game franchise Half-Life. Here is Kirk superimposed on the iconic McLovin ID from Superbad. “Kirkification”—and its syntactical variants—has gained real salience in the lexicon. You see it everywhere, with remarkable leaps in scale. For instance: Here is a Kirkified version of a scene from an Odyssey, where … well, where an unlucky soldier takes an arrow to the neck. (It’s sitting at 5.3 million views.) I could go on and on. Getting “Kirked” is now zoomer slang for getting killed. (It has also been deployed as a euphemism for oral sex. I don’t endorse any of this by the way—I am just reporting the facts.) Elsewhere, Kirk’s legacy has been desecrated to the point that it has shed even the thinnest connection to its original context. On a recent podcast clip, I heard the 22-year-old comedian and actress Ivy Wolk use the term “lowkirkuinely”—a portmanteau of “low-key” and “genuinely”—alloyed with the podcaster’s name. (“Just the name Charlie Kirk can send people of my generation into a fit of giggles,” said Wolk, explaining the humor.) You are free to find all of this to be pretty ghoulish. I can’t really blame you. Kirk left behind a widow and two young children. He was only 31 years old. The internet’s humor has a certain nihilism to it, and it’s fair for anyone to be turned off by that. (Remember, 9/11—which is also about to celebrate a big anniversary—is the subject of countless questionable memes as well.) But I do think something else is going on here, beyond typical online stuff. The derisiveness expressed toward Kirk—the permission so many people have given themselves to dance on his grave—feels, to me, in direct conversation with how Americans were instructed to process his untimely death, and that is one of the great unforced errors of the late and febrile Trump era. Seriously, remember what life felt like in the summer of 2025? It was the high-water mark of MAGAdom. Trump was back in office with his first and only popular vote victory under his belt. The tide of wokeness had been battled back into the abyss; companies and institutions cowered in front of this newly cemented moral majority. Liberals stared at themselves in the mirror, wondering how it all went so wrong, and some Democratic Party elites seemed eager to break right. (Here’s where I’ll remind you that shortly after the 2024 election, Gavin Newsom invited Kirk to his podcast studio.) So naturally, in the white heat of MAGA’s dominance, Kirk’s death resulted in him being feted like he was Nelson Mandela. Trump personally announced Kirk’s passing on his Twitter account and blessed him with the auspices of a state funeral. Republicans attempted to cobble together votes allowing the man to lie in state within the Capitol Rotunda; statues of Kirk were commissioned in MAGA strongholds; Vladimir Putin toasted his defense of “traditional values.” More importantly, the administration used Kirk to assert its political ambition, which we all thought was nearing its apogee. Stephen Miller announced a dubious crackdown on “far-left terror” networks, which felt like a naked extension of his paramilitary apparatus. And who could forget the whole Jimmy Kimmel debacle? The late-night host was yanked off TV for a spell, under direct pressure from the Federal Communications Commission—all because he had the temerity to make a mild joke at Kirk’s expense. It was those overreactions that, in retrospect, led to the latter-day blaspheming of Kirk—meted out in caustic memes, every day. Because no matter the horrific circumstances of his murder, Charlie Kirk is not Nelson Mandela. He went to his grave as the same man he always was: a bigoted podcaster who gleefully stoked hatred; someone who has antagonized innumerable minority groups, who helped bus rioters to the “Stop the Steal” rally, who, in fact, spent his last moments on Earth making a flagrantly racist comment about gang violence. None of that justified his fate—not even close—but it did make Kirk an easily recognizable archetype in the MAGA strata: an individual well-versed in the shabby right-wing grift, a gigantic microphone in front of his smirking face, permeating the airwaves with his poisonous worldview. The sacredness prescribed by the Trump administration—asking us to reserve the same solemnity for Kirk as we did for popes, firefighters, and veterans, which the president so rarely offers his own enemies—was a bridge too far. It turns out that millions of Americans refused to lie to themselves about who Kirk was. And as the MAGA machine attempted to inculcate respect for the departed podcaster through raw vehicles of coercion, the spirit of rebellion grew more potent. Ultimately, I think that is what a Kirkified meme attempts to express: the right to memorialize the man in any way we see fit. Two months after Kirk’s passing, a song titled “We Are Charlie Kirk” was uploaded by the YouTube channel “Authentic Music.” It was presented as a tribute to the late podcaster; the chorus speaks of “carrying the flame,” and “honoring his name.” It was also, clearly, the product of A.I., and like all A.I. music, the results were turgid, saccharine, and spiritually vacant—reminiscent of an Imagine Dragons B-side lost to time. Unsurprisingly, the song was quickly repurposed as a Kirk meme, and in that context, its lyrics have taken on a completely different meaning. The memers will ensure that the world never forgets who Charlie Kirk really was. They will shatter the MAGA propaganda machine. They will carry the flame. Shopify extends lifeline to Tailwind as vibe coding erodes web dev platform's bottom line
2026-09-10

Shopify extends lifeline to Tailwind as vibe coding erodes web dev platform's bottom line

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Acquisition gives open source CSS framework 'a stable long-term home' Tailwind CSS may still be open source, but it’s not independent anymore: It’s now a Shopify product. Tailwind Labs CEO Adam Wathan shared news of Shopify’s acquisition of his company in a blog post Thursday. Wathan said that he chose to sell to Shopify because the ecommerce platform was an early adopter of Tailwind CSS, and will serve as an ideal place for his open-source web development platform to “be developed in service of a real product.” “I believe deeply in Shopify's mission to help more people start, run, and grow their own business,” Wathan added. “Tailwind is a very important part of the stack at Shopify, and they're invested in making sure it's actively maintained and continues to improve and adapt for how the ways we build are changing.” For those unfamiliar with Tailwind CSS, it’s an open source framework that simplifies web development by providing utility classes that can be applied directly to elements in HTML documents. Traditional CSS, on the other hand, often involves maintaining separate stylesheets containing rules that are applied to HTML elements. It’s a useful tool, and one that’s installed over 110 million times a week, per Wathan, if you can believe that. But as we've seen several times, the rise of AI is making it hard for the platform's overlords to make ends meet. Wathan said in January that AI coding tools had started eating into Tailwind Labs' bottom line to the degree that he was forced to lay off three employees. He blamed the fact that vibe coding tools have begun to come between his product and potential customers, causing traffic to plummet and income to dry up. So Shopify has come to the rescue to save Tailwind CSS from languishing in maintenance hell like other open source projects. “We're joining Shopify to give Tailwind a stable long-term home where it will be actively maintained for the millions of people who depend on it,” Wathan explained in his blog post. The precise details depend on what type of Tailwind CSS user you are. “Nothing changes with Tailwind CSS or any of our other open-source projects,” Wathan said. “Everything will always be MIT-licensed, and our team will continue to lead and maintain these projects for the community with the support of Shopify.” But users of Tailwind’s premium services might find things changing, as Wathan said his team will no longer be trying to grow the business. That means Tailwind Plus, the company’s paid, UI-based package of templates and other tools, and ui.sh, Tailwind’s package of AI skills designed to help non-coders build user interfaces, are going the way of the dodo. “We're closing sign ups for new customers to focus on Tailwind CSS at Shopify,” Wathan explained. Existing customers who’ve paid for access to those tools and other paid Tailwind Labs offerings will still have access, Wathan stressed. We reached out to get more details on future support for those paid products, and will update this post if we hear back. ® War is peace. Freedom is slavery. Hundreds of AI agents helped PaperCut attacker hit 395+ orgs, and some went off script
2026-09-10

Hundreds of AI agents helped PaperCut attacker hit 395+ orgs, and some went off script

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Human operator: don't touch CIS orgs. AI agents: look a squirrel! An unknown attacker used hundreds of AI agents to exploit two PaperCut MF/NG bugs and break into at least 395 organizations. The victims were concentrated in the US education sector, and the intrusions moved fast. In one case, an American high school went from initial access to domain admin in seven minutes. These agents, powered by OpenAI’s Codex harness and a DeepSeek model, also allowed the miscreant to attack organizations at scale, according to threat-intel firm GreyNoise, which traced the campaign’s orchestration to 45.142.193.132 on August 31. “The adversary went from an empty workspace to first achieving RCE against a real victim in just under four hours, first domain admin in an additional two hours, and once the full campaign launched, compromised at least 11 organizations in 26 seconds,” GreyNoise analysts said in a Wednesday report. The security provider attributes these intrusions to a “likely Russian-speaking” criminal who used AI to develop exploits against the pair of PaperCut vulnerabilities disclosed just days earlier. On August 28, the print management software provider issued emergency patches for CVE-2026-81578 and CVE-2026-82078, at the time warning that it was “aware of confirmed customer incidents and are treating this matter with the highest priority.” The flaws affect PaperCut NG and MF, which are self-hosted Java web applications that by default run with SYSTEM-level privileges on Windows. PaperCut’s CEO later said that the first reported compromise came in on August 27, and involved an education-sector firm. On Thursday, PaperCut published security maintenance releases, which replace the earlier emergency fixes. By now, however, at least 440 instances hosted by 395 identified victim organizations in 48 countries have been compromised, according to GreyNoise. “There are other real victims that could not be attributed to a named organization,” the threat signals team wrote. The human attacker told the agents to avoid targeting entities in 28 countries with the top five being Russia, China, Hong Kong, Thailand, and Iran. Several Commonwealth of Independent States (CIS) countries are on the list, which is why GreyNoise says the crim is likely Russian-speaking. It’s typical for ransomware and other cybercrime operations to expressly avoid attacking Russia and other CIS countries, whose governments often provide safe harbor for extortionists and financially motivated crims - especially if they also happen to work day jobs as state-sponsored hackers. Plus, local cops tend to ignore the digital break-ins unless the gangs infect any in-country organizations. However, the agents in the PaperCut attacks didn’t always follow these instructions, and in some cases still hacked organizations based in countries on the do-not-hit list. “It’s currently uncertain why the [attacker's] agents deviated,” GreyNoise said. “But it is a good example of agents gone wild.” The US and the UK were the countries with the highest victim count, at 98 and 59, respectively. Schools and other education-industry organizations were, by far, the hardest hit with 204 victims. For comparison, the No. 2 industry (other/unclassified) had 51, while retail/commercial/professional services ranked third with 38 victims. After using AI to develop exploits, achieve remote code execution, and harvest credentials in a self-hosted lab, the baddie set hundreds of AI agents loose on the open internet to find and attack public-facing, vulnerable instances. “This campaign appears to be opportunistic,” according to GreyNoise. “There is a high concentration of US-based targets in the education sector; however, it’s likely that is more attributable to the customer base of PaperCut NG/MF.” Interestingly, the attacker did not immediately set to work on post-compromise evil deeds with all of the victims. GreyNoise noted “multiple-day delays” between gaining initial access and achieving domain admin “but only due to a lack of action by the adversary.” The fastest time was five minutes, while the longest was 144 minutes. It’s also unclear if the criminal is only focused on gaining access to compromised organizations - and then plans to hand the attack off to affiliates or other data-theft, extortion, and ransomware groups - or if they plan to use this access for follow-on nefarious activities of their own. GreyNoise does note that, in at least one case, Cloudflare’s Web Application Firewall (WAF) blocked the attacker. “Fundamental hardening of environments still matters against AI-enabled threats,” they wrote. It’s also worth noting that GreyNoise has been tracking malicious use of 45.142.193.132 since early July, and says this IP has been used in attacks against internet facing technologies and devices from Palo Alto, Ubiquiti, Citrix, SonicWall, and Proxmox VE. ® War is peace. NASA and IBM open source lunar mapping tools
2026-09-10

NASA and IBM open source lunar mapping tools

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Can help you pick the perfect spot for your future evil lair, or boffins make discoveries about Earth's satellite IBM and NASA have got together again and released an open source AI model of the Moon that could be used to make new discoveries about Earth’s natural satellite. The NASA‑IBM Lunar Foundation Model has been trained on an extensive lunar observation dataset curated by researchers at the two organizations, and is available now on Hugging Face. It is claimed as the first AI model to integrate observations captured in a range of modalities (data formats), and at different viewing angles and spatial scales. Instead of sifting through maps and images by hand or using low resolution machine learning models, scientists can use this to analyze geographic features, the pair say. In particular, NASA and IBM hope researchers will be able to discover previously unidentified lunar ice deposits, analyze volcanic features called Irregular Mare Patches, and identify and classify craters. Lunar ice indicates the presence of water and oxygen, which may be useful for future manned missions. It is found in permanently shadowed regions, which are among the most difficult areas to observe. The NASA-IBM model combines multimodal and multi-resolution observations to better predict where ice may be present on the lunar surface. Alongside the model, IBM and NASA scientists compiled an open-source lunar dataset from over 30 spatially-aligned layers, using data from nine instruments across four missions. It combines tens of thousands of images and maps showing various geophysical properties of the lunar surface. “NASA has spent decades building an extraordinary scientific record of the Moon, but collecting data is only part of the job,” said the space agency’s chief science data officer, Kevin Murphy.  “We also have to make data easier for scientists to explore and use.” “The NASA-IBM Lunar Foundation Model gives scientists a foundation to explore the Moon at scale, connecting observations across instruments, revealing patterns that are difficult to see in isolation, and providing an open platform the global research community can build on,” claimed IBM director of research for Europe, Juan Bernabe-Moreno. This isn’t the first such project the two organizations have worked on together. In 2023, the pair released Prithvi, an open-source foundation AI model to help scientists analyze satellite imagery. A year later, they released an AI climate model designed to accurately predict weather patterns, extending the Prithvi family of models. Last year, it was an AI model named Surya, developed to predict the kind of violent solar flare-ups that might disrupt satellites and spacecraft. This was also part of the Prithvi family, as is the Lunar Foundation Model. NASA and IBM have not officially disclosed a specific parameter count or exact model size for this latest release. As it is open-source and available to download, we asked what resources someone would need to use it. “Hardware needs will depend on the application, the size and number of inputs, and whether they’re running predictions or further training the model. As a rule of thumb, most of our fine-tuning experiments were conducted using Nvidia A100 GPUs,” an IBM spokesperson told us. “Smaller-scale experiments and inference workloads may be possible on more modest hardware, although the exact requirements will vary depending on the task.” Perhaps Reg readers will be able to make some discoveries using the new foundation model? Finding the craters made by rogue rocket stages, for example, or looking for evidence of little green men?® War is peace. Freedom is slavery. The Verge
Schools are catching on to Big Tech’s playbook
2026-09-10

Schools are catching on to Big Tech’s playbook

The Verge | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Educators were quick to adopt tech-backed coding curricula, but AI appears to face more scrutiny. Educators were quick to adopt tech-backed coding curricula, but AI appears to face more scrutiny. It’s the hot new thing in tech, and it’s where all the jobs are. Students who don’t learn to use it fall behind. And to help them catch up in time, its creators are graciously providing the resources and curriculum for learning it, often pro bono. That’s the narrative AI companies are pitching schools on right now, but according to New York Times education technology reporter Natasha Singer, it’s also how the tech industry has built its influence in classrooms for the past 15 years. In a new book, Coding Kids, Singer details how tech companies took a central role in promoting computers and coding skills in education, promising computer science skills were the ticket to high-paying jobs. But by the time many of those students entered the workforce, new technologies had thrown that promise into question. Meanwhile, the companies got schools to help them create a new class of would-be workers, and trained students on using their products and software, making them likely future customers. Now, the AI industry is pitching itself to schools with similar force. “I worry we have collective amnesia,” Singer told me on a recent episode of The Vergecast. “Have we learned any lessons from these past tech cycles in schools that are going to inform the rollout of AI, make sure that it benefits education, and put in the safeguards now — not a decade from now?” Fortunately, in some ways, she believes the answer is yes. Singer’s book recounts how more than a decade ago, the country’s largest tech companies embedded themselves directly into school curricula through courses for Advanced Placement Computer Science Principles classes. Apple and Microsoft each created their own curricula, featuring their own tools like Apple’s Swift or Microsoft’s Minecraft. “What’s striking about this is you can imagine that parents would not want, say, Pfizer AP Biology, or Northrop Grumman AP Physics, or ExxonMobil Environmental Science AP,” Singer says. She found that those in the medical field would likely understand why — medical students who are trained by a company on how to implant their proprietary device, one expert told her as an example, rarely choose to try another. Google has also made itself a dominant force in education through its distribution of low-cost laptops called Chromebooks. Singer tracked their ubiquitous adoption across schools through the 2010s, which she says was accelerated even further during the school shutdowns of the covid-19 pandemic. Google also introduced its Classroom app to help teachers communicate things like assignments and grades. By the time generative AI took off, Singer writes, Google was already a key platform provider for many schools, and well-positioned to promote it. Even when companies weren’t directly involved in education, they backed nonprofit groups like Code.org. Code.org kicked off its viral education campaign with a splashy celebrity-laden video that included tech moguls like Microsoft founder Bill Gates and then-Facebook founder Mark Zuckerberg, urging kids to learn to code. Code.org founder Hadi Partovi took a startup-style approach to promoting computer science education, and had little planned for how to capitalize on the attention of the video once it took off, Singer writes. He ultimately funneled that attention into a similarly splashy “Hour of Code” campaign across the country, urging schools to engage in entertaining hour-long courses introducing kids to coding building blocks. At the time, there wasn’t much pushback, Singer found — though there were some attempts at alternatives. One group of academics engaged in a more traditional, methodological approach to testing and rolling out their own computer science curriculum. It had the express goal of diversifying the kinds of students enrolled in such courses, and taught students to think about big questions around the internet and technology, and scrutinize builders’ motivations. Eventually, backlash arrived. Some recent graduates say they feel let down that they were told to code their whole lives, just to find such entry-level jobs to be diminishing, Singer told me. Some schools are getting rid of ubiquitous Google Chromebooks, and research around the world is finding that adding technology to the classroom often doesn’t have a significant impact on actual learning. “Most parents want more than just getting kids to learn to use tech” “I’m not arguing against tech education or tech in schools,” Singer says. “But what I am saying is most parents want more than just getting kids to learn to use tech. They want kids to learn how to be informed citizens in a democracy, and they want kids to figure out what their passions are and who they are and what their beliefs are… And so if we let the tech industry dictate or even heavily influence what happens in schools, then the question we’re asking is, how can we get AI into schools faster? Which is a product question, and not, ‘What are the most important things we want kids to learn?’” While the AI industry of the 2020s may be pulling from the 2010s playbook, there already appears to be one distinct difference: Parents and school districts are pushing back. Just last week, the nation’s largest school system, New York City, banned elementary and middle schools from letting students use AI in the classroom for the coming school year. A day later, Los Angeles announced an even broader restriction that included high schoolers. And unlike the few-and-far-between critiques Singer documented in the early days of tech’s coding push, there appears to be a vibrant grassroots movement fighting the screen- and AI-ification of the classroom among parents and teachers alike. “I think schools are beginning to figure it out,” says Singer, who’s “optimistic” that teachers and students are looking for a broader kind of tech education. That curriculum could teach kids “to ask deep questions about how companies are steering us with their technology, how software and AI are designed to affect our choices, and what role students have to use the tools productively. But also to push back if they like, and to not feel like they’re in a world where AI is driving them.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Electric air taxis get the green light for test flights in Texas
2026-09-10

Electric air taxis get the green light for test flights in Texas

The Verge | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
The flights will be conducted under the White House’s new pilot program to test out electric, hybrid, and autonomous aircraft. The flights will be conducted under the White House’s new pilot program to test out electric, hybrid, and autonomous aircraft. A new federal program to test the feasibility of electric, hybrid-electric, and autonomous aircraft kicks off today in Texas — before the rules governing this new technology have even been finalized. Earlier this year, the Federal Aviation Administration’s Advanced Air Mobility and Electric Vertical Takeoff and Landing (eVTOL) Integration Pilot Program (eIPP) selected eight state-led projects to serve as some of the first public tests of these new aircraft. The first one kicks off today in Texas, featuring three eVTOL companies: Joby, Beta, and Wisk. For the next week, these companies will conduct test flights demonstrating future routes between cities and regional airports, including Dallas Fort Worth International Airport. The aircraft won’t carry any passengers, but regulators will be keen to see how the state’s air traffic control system handles the uptick in flight traffic. The companies, meanwhile, are eager to prove to the federal government that their aircraft deserve to be awarded type certification, which is required before any of them can carry passengers commercially in the US. But type certification can take years to attain, which led to the White House to create this pilot program allowing these companies to demonstrate their new technology — even before the regulatory process has concluded. Through the eIPP, the companies hope to accelerate their path to market by getting a head start on data collection that will be needed for certification. Data from the program will also be used to “develop new regulations that safely enable this futuristic technology at scale,” the White House said earlier. The companies say they are thrilled to demonstrate the future of electric and autonomous flight, which they argue will help reduce car traffic in cities and open up new mobility opportunities for consumers. “I’m incredibly excited by the potential for vertical lift in Texas, a state with the infrastructure, expertise, and partnerships to help lead the next era of American aviation,” JoeBen Bevirt, Founder and CEO of Joby Aviation, said in a statement. Several of these companies have conducted public test flights before — Joby’s egg-shaped aircraft demonstrated its future route between Lower Manhattan and JFK Airprot earlier this year — but these will be the first official flights under the White House’s new pilot program. Additional tests are being planned in New York, Utah, Pennsylvania, Louisiana, Florida, North Carolina, and New Mexico. Legal in-fighting, however, could muddy the picture for electric aviation. Joby and one of its rivals, Archer, are currently suing each other for stolen trade secrets and corporate espionage. Technology limitations, including battery weight and sensor decisions, could also hamper the rollout. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Wolverine on the PS5 goes back to a simpler (and bloodier) style of action game
2026-09-10

Wolverine on the PS5 goes back to a simpler (and bloodier) style of action game

The Verge | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
The next few months are packed with new games, and PlayStation goes old-school to cut through the noise. Marvel’s Wolverine captures just how angry its lead character is. The latest PS5 exclusive from Spider-Man developer Insomniac, Wolverine is a straightforward action game that is at its best when you’re (literally) slicing through enemies or thrust into a sequence pulled from a blockbuster movie, like climbing the fuselage of a plane as it careens toward land or racing a motorcycle across rain-slicked city streets. It’s the kind of game that can really get your heart rate rising, and keeping Logan mad is a key part of the strategy. In many ways, it’s a throwback to straightforward action titles of the past — and it’s a refreshing change of pace in a medium saturated with live-service and open-world games. Though it comes from Insomniac, Wolverine is very different from Spider-Man, which thrust the web-slinger into an open-world version of New York City. Instead, this is a much more linear experience that feels like a combination of two other PlayStation staples: God of War and Uncharted. Rather than follow Wolverine as a member of the X-Men, the story instead has him as an often reluctant member of Team X, a mercenary group with similar pro-mutant aims but a much more violent approach. Instead of heroes like Cyclops and Professor X, Wolvy is joined by more villain-adjacent characters like Sabretooth, Mystique, and Nathaniel Essex, before eventually connecting with Jean Grey. The story pulls Logan and his companions across the globe, but it starts out relatively simple, as they investigate a rash of mutant disappearances connected to a billionaire arms dealer. The most important thing about both Wolverine the character and Wolverine the game is fighting. This is a hairy little dude who loves to scrap, and his mutant powers — healing and sharp claws — make it so that he is very good at it, able to both take a huge amount of damage and dish it out. And the best part of the game is how Wolverine-specific the action is. The combat is fast and violent and forces you to be aggressive. Wolverine can leap huge distances to take out foes and use his claws to both repel attacks and slice at enemies, often with very gruesome results. (At the end of a battle he’s usually coated in blood.) An important part of this is Wolverine’s rage level, which increases the more you fight. As his rage grows, it allows you to do two things: go absolutely berserk and kill a rash of enemies with a single blow, and use your healing power as a sort of extra life in case your health depletes. It’s a clever system that means you can’t sit back and play it safe. Wolverine isn’t chill, and you can’t be either. That’s the God of War side of Wolverine. The Uncharted side comes in the form of all of the over-the-top set-pieces you’re thrown into. I’ll refrain from detailing some of the best ones for spoiler reasons, but I’ll say that, just like in Uncharted, these moments make it feel like you’re playing out a wild sequence from an action movie. There are chases across the rooftops of Tokyo, massive boss battles against some iconic comic book villains, and scenes where Wolverine is literally being ripped apart as he fights for survival. The flip side is that, because of this setup, Wolverine is also much more restrictive than most of its contemporaries. In some ways this is a good thing; it’s refreshing to play a game that isn’t trying to do everything. But there are still some awkward elements. For instance, much like in Spider-Man, there are lots of collectibles that will reveal new bits of story or unlock new suits for Wolverine to wear. But exploration outside of the main path is extremely dull, so the only reason to track these down is if you have a completionist mindset. It’s also disappointing that Wolverine’s closet of outfits is much less fun than Spider-Man’s, and they often feel out of place with the game’s attempt at a more gritty tone. Making matters worse is that, despite a promising start, the story ends up in a largely forgettable place, with few surprises, particularly if you have an even slight knowledge of the X-Men universe from comics or the animated series. Outside of combat and set-pieces, Wolverine skews generic AAA game; the voice acting, the cutscenes, and even some of the missions feel like standard video game fare, many of which seem out of place. Wolverine is amazing when you’re surrounded by ninjas trying to kill you. But when Logan is in a tux trying to spy on a fancy party? Not so much. All that said, Wolverine’s return to a simpler and smaller style of action — I beat the game in around 13 hours — is still notable, particularly from Sony, which has had a rough few years after placing many ill-fated bets in the live-service space. But much like last year’s Ghost of Yōtei, Wolverine shows that PlayStation is still capable of the single-player blockbusters the platform is best known for. It’s also launching at perhaps the busiest video game release period in recent memory, going up against major titles including Onimusha: Way of the Sword, Control Resonant, Fire Emblem: Fortune’s Weave, Gears of War: E-Day, Silent Hill: Townfall, Star Wars: Zero Company, and, soon enough, The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time and Grand Theft Auto VI. In that company, despite occasionally skewing generic, Wolverine’s bloodthirsty focus on action is something that makes it stand out. Marvel’s Wolverine launches on September 15th on the PS5.
